: To assign revenues to each of the products, we first sort the products by the attraction parameters that were obtained from the maximum likelihood estimation. We then generate random revenues
r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}
uniformly over
\{1,\ldots,10000\}
. These revenues are then assigned to the attraction parameters in ascending order, with the lowest revenue being assigned to the highest attraction parameter, the second lowest revenue being assigned to the second-highest attraction parameter, and so on.
11
11

11

After performing Step 3, the attraction parameters in the underlying multinomial logit model satisfy
\nu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{N}
and the revenues of the products satisfy
r_{1}\leq\cdots\leq r_{N}
. We perform this step in order to make the downstream assortment optimization problem less trivial, since if high-revenue products also have high values for attraction parameters, then optimal assortments are likely to simply contain the highest revenue products.

•

Step 4
: To draw a sample from the multinomial logit model with rank cutoffs, we first draw the utilities
U_{1},\ldots,U_{N+1}
as independent Gumbel random variables with the attraction parameters obtained from the maximum likelihood estimation in Step 2. If
|\{i\in[N]:U_{i}>U_{N+1}\}|>L
, then we increase
U_{N+1}
so that it is the
(L+1)
th highest utility.

To solve the resulting assortment optimization problem with the sample average approximation, we follow the process outlined Section
2.2
. That is, we first select an integer
\tilde{K}
, and then we generate the set of
\tilde{K}
samples using the process in Step 4. To evaluate the out-of-sample performance of assortments obtained by the sample average approximation, we independently generate a validation set of
10^{4}
samples using the same process as in Step 4 (see Section
5.1.3
).

Bai et al. 2024
developed an algorithm for these assortment optimization problems that finds an
(1-\epsilon)
-factor approximate solution that runs in
\mathcal{O}((N/\epsilon)^{\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon^{2})})
time. Because we are interested in computing approximate solutions that are within several percentage points of optimal for problems with moderate-to-large numbers of products
N
, we focus in the following numerical experiments on methods based on the sample average approximation. Specifically, we compare the algorithms proposed in this paper for solving the sample average approximation to those from
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
.

5.1.2
Experiment 1.

In our first set of experiments, we investigate a setting of assortment optimization problems in which there is a moderate number of products,
N=50
, and where we choose a very large number of samples
\tilde{K}
with the goal of the sample average approximation producing an assortment that is close to optimal. This setting is essentially that described in Section
3.2
in which the exclusion set formulation is intended to be efficacious.

Table
5.1.2
compares the computation times for three different solution methods for the sample average approximation. The first solution method is our exclusion set formulation (
12
) from Section
3
. The second solution method is our accelerated Benders decomposition method from Section
4
. The third solution method is the mixed-integer programming formulation (
8
) from
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
. We solve (
8
) from Section
3.1.1
instead of their original formulation (
4
) because the multinomial logit model with rank-cutoffs leads to small consideration sets, which makes the original formulation (
4
) much slower to solve. Table
5.1.2
does not compare to the original two-phase Benders decomposition method by
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
from Section
2.4.2
because it is slower than our accelerated Benders decomposition method (see Sections
5.1.3
and
5.2
), which itself is slower than the exclusion set formulation in Table
5.1.2
. All results in Table
5.1.2
are averaged over ten replications.

4
(ABD). Results are averaged over ten replications and rounded to two decimal places.

There are several main observations from Table
5.1.2
. First, we observe that the relative speedups of the exclusion set formulation (
12
) compared to the other solution methods increases with the number of samples
\tilde{K}
. For example, when
M=5
, the ratio of computation times of the existing mixed-integer programming formulation (
8
) to the exclusion set formulation (
12
) increases from a ratio of
23.01/3.45\approx 6.67
(in the case of
\tilde{K}=2.5\times 10^{4}
) to a ratio of
356.43/19.50\approx 18.27
(in the case of
\tilde{K}=10^{5}
). This can be attributed to the fact that the number of ‘collisions’ of rankings in the exclusion set formulation increases as the number of samples
\tilde{K}
increases.
12
12

12

The notion of collisions is found in Section
3.2
.
This explanation is corroborated by Table
5.1.2
, which shows that increasing the number of samples
\tilde{K}
leads to larger ratio in problem sizes between (
12
) and (
8
). For example, when
M=5
, Table
5.1.2
shows that the ratio of the number of decision variables of the existing mixed-integer programming formulation (
8
) to the exclusion set formulation (
12
) increases from a ratio of
121537/41933\approx 2.9
(in the case of
\tilde{K}=2.5\times 10^{4}
) to a ratio of
472752/117683\approx 4.0
(in the case of
\tilde{K}=10^{5}
). A large number of collisions not only implies that (
12
) has a smaller problem size than (
8
); it also implies that (
12
) generally has a tighter linear programming relaxation than (
8
).
13
13

13

Additional results regarding the linear programming relaxation gaps of (
8
) and (
12
) can be found in Appendix
K.3
.

Table
5.1.2
also highlights the impact of the spread of the attraction parameters in the multinomial logit model. As discussed in Section
5.1.1
, larger choices of
M
generally lead to a lower spread of the attraction parameters. Indeed, we observe from Table
5.1.2
that the ratio of computation times of the existing mixed-integer programming formulation (
8
) to the exclusion set formulation (
12
) increases as
M
decreases, everything else held constant. This observation is further corroborated by comparing the ratios of problem sizes of (
12
) and (
8
) in Table
5.1.2
. We emphasize that even in the case of
M=5
, the resulting attraction parameters are still close enough for the problem to be nontrivial, in the sense that all
N
products still appear among the
L=5
most preferred products in at least some of the generated samples; see Appendix
K.2
for details.

In summary, this first set of experiments shows that the exclusion set formulation can offer significant speedups for solving the sample average approximation, where the speedups are most pronounced in settings with large numbers of samples. This showcases the value of the exclusion set formulation in applications with small consideration sets, particularly in realistic settings where some products appear in consideration sets more often than others. We note that the aforementioned tables compare the solution methods in settings without constraints on the set of feasible assortments; additional experiments for the exclusion set formulation for settings with cardinality constraints on the set of feasible assortments can be found in
Appendix
K.3
.

5.1.3
Experiment 2.

In our second set of experiments, we investigate assortment optimization problems in which there are larger numbers of products,
N\in\{100,500\}
, and where the rank cutoff satisfies
L\in\{10,15\}
. This setting contrasts to that of Section
5.1.2
because the inflated size of the assortment optimization problems in the present Section
5.1.3
prevents the formulations (
12
) and (
8
) from being solvable in practical computation times when the number of samples
\tilde{K}
is large. This second set of experiments also provides a natural setting for empirically assessing the out-of-sample performance of assortments obtained by the sample average approximation as a function of the number of samples
\tilde{K}
in problems with hundreds of products.

.

. The results in Table

As discussed in Section

, the improved computation times of our accelerated Benders decomposition method can be attributed to two factors: our faster algorithms for generating cuts, and the strength of the generated cuts. To better understand the role of those two factors in driving the speedups of the accelerated Benders decomposition method in Table
5.1.3
, we present granular results for the two solution methods in Tables

and
5.1.3
. Table

shows the average computation time for each solution method for Phase 1 and Phase 2, and Table
5.1.3
shows the average number of cuts generated by each solution method in Phase 1 and Phase 2. These tables show significant reductions in computation time and in number of generated cuts by our accelerated Benders decomposition method in both phases. In particular, we draw several insights from Tables
5.1.3
and
5.1.3
.

The first insight is that the speedups of the accelerated Benders decomposition method in Phase 1 can be attributed in large part to the reduction in computation times for generating cuts. Specifically, Table
5.1.3
shows that the ratios of computation times of Phase 1 of the original two-phase Benders decomposition method to Phase 1 of our accelerated Benders decomposition method range from
134.04/12.18\approx 11
(in the case of
N=100
and
\tilde{K}=10000
) to
4464.64/2.68\approx 1666
(in the case of
N=500
and
\tilde{K}=20000
). These ratios in computation times are much larger than the ratios in the number of cuts generated in Phase 1 between the two solution methods shown in Table
5.1.3
, which range from
7686/2609\approx 2.95
(in the case of
N=500
and
\tilde{K}=2500
) to
66355/21402\approx 3.10
(in the case of
N=100
and
\tilde{K}=20000
). Furthermore, it follows from Table
5.1.3
that in the case of
N=500
, the total computation times in Table
5.1.3
is primarily driven by the computation time of Phase 1. These results underscore the value of our faster algorithms from Section
4.2
for generating cuts, which reduced the computation time in Phase 1 from
\mathcal{O}(KN^{2})
to
\mathcal{O}(\sum_{k=1}^{K}L_{k}\log L_{k})
.

The second insight is that the improved strength of our generated cuts in the accelerated Benders decomposition method also plays a significant role in reducing the overall computation time compared to the original two-phase Benders decomposition method. Indeed, Table
5.1.3
shows a significant reduction in the accelerated Benders decomposition method in the total number of cuts generated across Phase 1 and Phase 2 compared to the original two-phase Benders decomposition method. This difference is seen in Table
5.1.3
most prominently in Phase 2, which reflects the strength of the cuts from both Phase 1 and Phase 2. For example, when
N=500
and
\tilde{K}=2500
, the number of Phase 2 cuts reduces from 3267 in the original two-phase Benders decomposition method to 14 in the accelerated Benders decomposition method.

It is worthwhile to make a couple of additional comments about Table
5.1.3
and our implementation of the solution methods. First, we note that the numbers of cuts shown in this table refers only to the number of generated cuts, i.e., it does not include the number of initial cuts. To avoid unboundedness of the outer problem (
5
), both of the two solution methods initialize the outer problem (
5
) in Phase 1 with a single constraint for each ranking (see Appendix
K.1.3
for details). In both solution methods, these initial as well as the generated cuts in Phase 1 are passed as warm starts into Phase 2. Second, we observe that the number of cuts in Phase 1 in Table
5.1.3
for the accelerated Benders decomposition method is typically close to the number of samples
\tilde{K}
. This suggests that only a small number of cuts were actually required in these experiments to determine the optimal solution for the linear programming relaxation of (
5
) that is solved in Phase 1. However, this fact is not identified by the original two-phase Benders decomposition method because its cuts are weaker, as demonstrated by the high number of cuts for Phase 1 of that solution method in Table
5.1.3
. This underscores the value of the Pareto-optimal cuts that are generated by our accelerated Benders decomposition method.

We conclude by calculating the approximation gap of the assortments obtained by the sample average approximation. Given any assortment
S\in\mathscr{S}
, let
J^{*}(S)\triangleq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in S}r_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{U_{i}=\max_{j\in S}U_{j}\right\}\right]
denote the expected revenue, and let
v^{*}\triangleq\sup_{S\in\mathscr{S}}J^{*}(S)
denote the optimal objective value of the stochastic program (
1
). Let
\hat{v}_{\tilde{K}}
and
\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}}
denote the optimal objective value and optimal solution of the sample average approximation (
2
), which is shown in Sections
2.3
and
2.4.2
to be equivalent to the outer problem (
5
). We recall that the optimal objective value of the sample average approximation
\hat{v}_{\tilde{K}}
is an upward biased estimate of the optimal objective value of the stochastic program
v^{*}

(
Shapiro et al. 2021
, Proposition 5.6)
. Moreover, an unbiased estimate of
J^{*}(\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}})
can be obtained by using Monte-Carlo simulation to generate an out-of-sample validation set of samples
(\breve{U}^{\tilde{k}}_{1},\ldots,\breve{U}^{\tilde{k}}_{N+1})
for
\tilde{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\breve{K}\}
and calculating
\breve{J}_{\breve{K}}(\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}})\triangleq\frac{1}{\breve{K}}\sum_{\tilde{k}=1}^{\breve{K}}\sum_{i\in\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}}}r_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{\breve{U}^{\tilde{k}}_{i}=\max_{j\in\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}}}\breve{U}^{\tilde{k}}_{j}\right\}
. A lower bound estimate of the approximation gap
J^{*}(\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}})/v^{*}\times 100\%
for the sample average approximation (
2
) is thus given by
\breve{J}_{\tilde{K}^{\prime}}(\hat{S}_{\tilde{K}})/\hat{v}_{\tilde{K}}\times 100\%
.

Table
5.1.3
reports the lower bound estimate of the approximation gap of the assortments obtained from solving the sample average approximation, using a validation set of
\breve{K}=10000
samples. As expected, the results show that as the number of products
N
and the rank cutoff
L
increase, a larger number of samples
\tilde{K}
are required for the sample average approximation (
2
) to yield assortments that are close to optimal with respect to the stochastic program (
1
). That said, Tables
5.1.3
and
5.1.3
together show for these experiments that assortments that are within
90\%
of optimal can be found within one minute. This underscores the value of our accelerated Benders decomposition method, and the potential of the sample average approximation more generally, for solving assortment optimization problems with hundreds of products.

5.2
Experiments with Real Data

In this section, we evaluate the performance of our accelerated Benders decomposition method (Section
4
) and the original two-phase Benders decomposition method of
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
(Section
2.4.2
) using a widely studied dataset from
Toubia et al. 2003
. This setting is valuable for three reasons. First, it represents a real-world application, where the samples are generated using individual-level questionnaire responses designed from conjoint analysis. As such, this setting is representative of the complexity and scale typically encountered in practical assortment optimization problems, even though the samples in this dataset are obtained by surveys instead of Monte-Carlo simulation. Second, in contrast to the experiments from Section
5.1
, the samples in this real-world dataset do not have small consideration sets, which provides a different setting for exploring the efficacy of the accelerated Benders decomposition method. Third, this dataset has been used by
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
to benchmark their own Benders decomposition method due to its large size (
N=3584,\tilde{K}=330)
, thereby offering an established point of comparison for assessing the practical efficiency of our accelerated Benders decomposition method. Additional numerical results on the out-of-sample performance of the sample average approximation on the dataset from
Toubia et al. 2003
can be found in Appendix
K.4
.

).
In the particular case of the cardinality constraint of five products (
\sum_{i}x_{i}=5
), our accelerated Benders decomposition method reduces the computation time from around six minutes to around 18 seconds. Tables
5.2
and
5.2
compares the computation times and number of generated cuts of the two solution methods separated by Phase 1 and Phase 2. These tables show that the speedups from the accelerated Benders decomposition method can be attributed in large part to our faster algorithm for generating cuts in Phase 1. In particular, the time to generate cuts in Phase 1 for our accelerated Benders decomposition method remained less than five seconds across all budget settings, demonstrating that our accelerated Benders decomposition method can rapidly generate high quality upper bounds.

In summary, these experiments show the capability of our accelerated Benders decomposition method in solving real-world large-scale problems without any heuristics or parameter tuning. We emphasize that although this setting has considerably fewer rankings than products, we show using cross validation in Appendix
K.4
that the relatively small number of samples is sufficient in this real-world dataset to obtain a near-optimal assortment for the true assortment optimization problem (
1
). As such, these experiments show the potential of the sample average approximation, in combination with our algorithms, in obtaining near-optimal solutions to problems with thousands of products.

6
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we make the sample average approximation a more viable approach to solving assortment optimization problems by developing faster algorithms for solving assortment optimization problems under the ranking-based choice model. We developed our algorithms by drawing connections between several streams of literature, ranging from Pareto optimality in Benders decomposition
(
Magnanti and Wong 1981
)
to mechanism design
(
Ma 2023
)
and isotonic regression
(
Ahuja and Orlin 2001
)
. We believe that our algorithms and their accompanying theoretical guarantees provide new compelling evidence that the sample average approximation can offer value to industry, allowing firms to focus on the task of estimating accurate discrete choice models. These results also raise a number of interesting directions for future work on the theory and practice of assortment optimization.

1.

Theoretical convergence rates:
It is well known for stochastic discrete optimization problems that the probability that the optimal solution set of the sample average approximation is a subset of the optimal solution set of the stochastic program converges to one exponentially fast as the number of samples tends to infinity
(
Kleywegt et al. 2002
, Section 2.2)
. To the best of our knowledge, it is unknown for the particular application of assortment optimization (
1
) whether the sample average approximation (
2
) converges even faster to (
1
), even in simple settings where the utilities
U_{1},\ldots,U_{N+1}
are independent Gumbel random variables. Future work on refined convergence rates would lead to an improved theoretical understanding of the settings where (
2
) can provide a close approximation of (
1
) with small or moderate numbers of samples.

2.

Faster algorithms under independent random utilities.
Much of the assortment optimization literature has focused on random utility maximization models where the random utilities
U_{1},\ldots,U_{N+1}
are independent random variables. At the same time, these problems are known to be computationally demanding, even in the simple case where the utilities are independent exponential random variables
(
Aouad et al. 2023
)
. An interesting direction for future work would be to develop faster Monte Carlo-based algorithms for solving (
1
) that are tailored to the setting where the utilities
U_{1},\ldots,U_{N+1}
are independent random variables.

3.

Faster algorithms for non-rational choice models
. The accelerated Benders decomposition from Section
4
introduces new connections between isotonic regression and develops structural results for Pareto-optimal cuts. These insights may prove to be useful in the development of Benders decomposition methods for classes of assortment optimization problems under irrational choice models; see
Chen and Mišić 2022
and
Akchen and Mišić 2021
.

\ECSwitch

Appendix A
Additional details from Section
2

A.1
Equivalence of MIP formulations

Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
present the same mixed-integer programming formulation of (
3
), with the exception that the constraints

\displaystyle x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[N+1]

(
4c
)

from the mixed-integer program (
4
) are replaced in
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
by

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{N+1}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}\leq 1-x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[N+1]

We observe that these two sets of constraints are equivalent because constraints (
4b
) and (
4c
) imply that
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{N+1}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}=1-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}
for all
k\in[K]
and
\ell\in[N+1]
.

A.2
Benders decomposition method from
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019

As discussed in Section
2.4.2
, the Benders decomposition method from
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
consists of two phases. In the first phase, Benders decomposition is used to solve the linear programming relaxation of (
5
). In the second phase, Benders decomposition is used to solve (
5
), which is warm started with the cuts obtained from the first phase. Each iteration of Phase 1 and Phase 2 of the Benders decomposition method of
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
requires solving the linear program (
7
) to optimality for each ranking
k
.

In greater detail, the Benders decomposition method consists of the following two phases.

Phase 1:

In the first phase of the Benders decomposition method, we solve the linear programming relaxation of (
5
) using constraint generation. Indeed, it follows from strong duality that the linear programming relaxation of (
5
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underset{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c},{\bm{q}}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda_{k}q_{k}

(17)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle q_{k}\leq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}

where we recall from Section
2.4.2
that
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is the set of feasible solutions for (
7
). In each iteration of the constraint generation method in Phase 1, we solve a relaxation of (
17
) obtained by, for each ranking
k\in[K]
, replacing
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with a finite subset
\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{k}\subset\mathcal{D}_{k}
. Given the optimal solution
(\hat{{\bm{x}}},\hat{{\bm{q}}})
for that relaxation, we then loop over the rankings, and, for each ranking
k
, we solve (
7
) to obtain a vector
(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{k},\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}_{k},\hat{\gamma}_{k})\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
that satisfies
\rho_{k}(\hat{{\bm{x}}})=\hat{\gamma}_{k}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}(\hat{\alpha}_{k,\ell}-\hat{\beta}_{k,\ell})\hat{x}_{i_{k,\ell}}
. If
\hat{q}_{k}\leq\rho_{k}(\hat{{\bm{x}}})
for every ranking
k\in[K]
, then we conclude that
(\hat{{\bm{x}}},\hat{{\bm{q}}})
is an optimal solution for (
17
). Otherwise, for each ranking that satisfies
\hat{q}_{k}>\rho_{k}(\hat{{\bm{x}}})
, we add
(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}_{k},\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}_{k},\hat{\gamma}_{k})
into
\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
, and we then continue to the next iteration.

Phase 2:

In the second phase of the Benders decomposition method, we solve (
5
) using constraint generation, which is warm-started using the constraints generated in Phase 1. Indeed, it follows from strong duality that (
5
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underset{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X},{\bm{q}}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lambda_{k}q_{k}

(18)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle q_{k}\leq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}

Similarly as in Phase 1, each iteration of the constraint generation method for Phase 2 consists of solving (
18
), followed by solving (
7
) for each ranking
k
to see if there is a
({\bm{\alpha}}_{k},{\bm{\beta}}_{k},\gamma_{k})\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
for which the constraints are violated. If so, we add the violated constraints and solve again; otherwise, we conclude that the current integer solution is an optimal solution for (
5
). The purpose of the first phase is to generate constraints to warm start the constraint generation method for solving (
18
).

Remark A.1

Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
shows that the constraint generation methods for Phase 1 and Phase 2 are guaranteed to converge after finite numbers of iterations and output optimal solutions for (
17
) and (
18
), respectively. We note that constraint generation in Phase 2 can implemented using lazy constraints within branch-and-bound; see
Bertsimas and Mišić 2019
.

Appendix B
Omitted Details from Section
3

B.1
Proof of Lemma
3.1

Proof B.1

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.
Consider any
k,k^{\prime}\in[K]
,
L\in[L_{k}]
, and suppose that
\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}=\{i_{k^{\prime},1},\ldots,i_{k^{\prime},L}\}
. It follows from the fact that
L\in[L_{k}]
that
i_{k,\ell}<N+1
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, which implies that
i_{k^{\prime},\ell}<N+1
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. We thus conclude that
L\leq L_{k^{\prime}}
, which implies that (
9
) contains the constraints
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}\geq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k^{\prime},\ell}
and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k^{\prime},\ell}\geq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}
.

\halmos

B.2
Strength of formulation (
11
)

In the following example, we show that the linear programming relaxation of (
11
) can be strictly tighter than the linear programming relaxation of (
8
). Indeed, consider an example in which there are
N=3
products and
K=2
rankings. The two rankings are given by

\displaystyle i_{1,1}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle i_{2,1}

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle i_{1,2}

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle i_{2,2}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle i_{1,3}

\displaystyle=3,

\displaystyle i_{2,3}

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle i_{1,4}

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle i_{2,4}

\displaystyle=3,

the probabilities of the two rankings are
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0.5
, and the revenues of the products are
r_{1}=r_{2}=\$100
, and
r_{3}=\$150
. For this example, we observe that (
8
) is

\displaystyle\underset{{\bm{x}}\in\{0,1\}^{3},{\bm{y}}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\left(100y_{1,1}+100y_{1,2}+150y_{1,3}\right)\times 0.5+\left(100y_{2,1}+100y_{2,2}\right)\times 0.5

(19)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &y_{1,1}+y_{1,2}+y_{1,3}\leq 1\\
&y_{2,1}+y_{2,2}\leq 1\\
&x_{1}\leq y_{1,1}\\
&x_{2}\leq y_{1,1}+y_{1,2}\\
&x_{3}\leq y_{1,1}+y_{1,2}+y_{1,3}\\
&x_{2}\leq y_{2,1}\\
&x_{1}\leq y_{2,1}+y_{2,2}\\
&0\leq y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}&&\forall k\in\{1,2\},\ell\in[L_{k}]\end{aligned}

We now make several claims.

Claim 1

The optimal objective value of (
19
) is equal to
\$100
and
{\bm{x}}\in\{0,1\}^{3}
is an optimal solution for (
19
) if and only if
x_{1}=1
or
x_{2}=1
.

Proof B.2

Proof of Claim
1
.
Suppose that
x_{1}=x_{2}=0
. Then we observe that the revenue from the second ranking is equal to $0 (because the second ranking prefers the no-purchase option 4 to product 3) and that the revenue from the first ranking is at most
\$150
. However, if
x_{1}=1
or
x_{2}=1
, then the revenue we get from the first ranking is
\$100
and the revenue we get from the second ranking is
\$100
. Since
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0.5
, then we conclude that every optimal solution of (
19
) must satisfy
x_{1}=1
or
x_{2}=1
and that the optimal objective value of (
19
) is
\$100
.

\halmos

Claim 2

The optimal objective value of the linear programming relaxation of (
19
) is equal to
\$112.5
and
{\bm{x}}\in[0,1]^{3}
is an optimal solution for the linear programming relaxation of (
19
) if and only if
x_{1}=x_{2}=0.5
and
x_{3}\geq 0.5
.

Proof B.3

Proof of Claim
2
.
While this claim can be verified numerically, it is useful to sketch out the intuition. Indeed, we observe for all
{\bm{x}}\in[0,1]^{3}
that every feasible solution for the linear programming relaxation of
(
19
) must satisfy
y_{2,1}=x_{2}
. Therefore, we observe that every optimal solution will satisfy
y_{2,2}=\min\{x_{1},1-y_{2,1}\}=\min\{x_{1},1-x_{2}\}
, and so the revenue from the second ranking will be

\displaystyle 100y_{2,1}+100y_{2,2}=100x_{2}+100\min\{x_{1},1-x_{2}\}=100\min\{x_{1}+x_{2},1\}

We further observe that if
x_{1}=x_{2}=0.5
and
x_{3}\geq 0.5
, then there exists a feasible solution in which
y_{1,1}=0.5
,
y_{1,2}=0
, and
y_{1,3}=0.5
, and so the revenue from the first ranking will be

\displaystyle 100y_{1,1}+100y_{1,2}+150y_{1,3}=125

We observe that increasing
x_{1}=0.5
or
x_{2}=0.5
by
\epsilon>0
will result in the revenue from the first ranking decreasing by
150\epsilon-100\epsilon=50\epsilon
and the revenue from the second product being unchanged. Conversely, decreasing
x_{1}=0.5
or
x_{2}=0.5
by
\epsilon>0
will result in the revenue from the first ranking increasing by at most
150\epsilon-100\epsilon=50\epsilon
and the revenue from the second ranking decreasing by
100\epsilon
. We thus conclude that
x_{1}=0.5
and
x_{2}=0.5
is optimal, and we readily observe that every optimal solution will satisfy
x_{3}\geq 0.5
.

\halmos

If follows from Claims
1
and
2
that the linear programming relaxation of (
19
) has an optimal objective value (
0.5\times\$125+0.5\times\$100=\$112.5
) that is strictly greater than the optimal objective value of (
19
) (
\$100
). Moreover, it follows from Claim
2
that every optimal solution for the linear programming relaxation of (
19
) is violated by the constraint
y_{1,1}+y_{1,2}=y_{2,1}+y_{2,2}
. We have thus shown for this example that the linear programming relaxation of (
11
) is tighter than the linear programming relaxation of (
8
).

B.3
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Proof B.4

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Recall the optimization problem (
11
), repeated below for convenience.

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N},\textbf{y}\end{subarray}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}y_{k,\ell}\lambda_{k}

(
11
)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k^{\prime},\ell}&&\forall k,k^{\prime}\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]:\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}=\{i_{k^{\prime},1},\ldots,i_{k^{\prime},L}\}\\
&\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}y_{k,\ell}\leq 1&&\forall k\in[K]\\
&x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\\
&0\leq y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\end{aligned}

We observe that we can, without loss of generality, introduce a decision variable
z_{E}
into (
11
) with the constraint that
z_{E}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}
for all
k\in[K]
and
L\in[L_{k}]
that satisfy
E=\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}
. Note that adding these decision variables and constraints into (
11
) is without loss of generality because the constraint
\sum_{\ell=1}^{|E|}y_{k,\ell}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{|E|}y_{k^{\prime},\ell}
is imposed for all
k,k^{\prime}
that satisfy
E=\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,|E|}\}=\{i_{k^{\prime},1},\ldots,i_{k^{\prime},|E|}\}
. Therefore, the above optimization problem can be written as

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N},\textbf{y},{\mathbf{z}}\end{subarray}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}y_{k,\ell}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}=z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}}&&\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]\\
&\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}y_{k,\ell}\leq 1&&\forall k\in[K]\\
&x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\\
&0\leq y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\end{aligned}

We next observe that the nonnegativity of the decision variables
y_{k,\ell}
and the constraints
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}y_{k,\ell}\leq 1
together imply that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}\leq 1
for all
k\in[K]
and
L\in[L_{k}]
, and so the above problem is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N},\textbf{y},{\mathbf{z}}\end{subarray}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}y_{k,\ell}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}=z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}}&&\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]\\
&\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}\leq 1&&\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]\\
&x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\\
&0\leq y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\end{aligned}

It follows from substitution that the above problem is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N},\textbf{y},{\mathbf{z}}\end{subarray}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}y_{k,\ell}\lambda_{k}

(20)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}=z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}}&&\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]\\
&z_{\left\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\right\}}\leq 1&&\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]\\
&x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\\
&0\leq y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\end{aligned}

We also observe that the constraints

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}y_{k,\ell}=z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\}}\quad\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]

can be rewritten equivalently as

\displaystyle z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}-z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}}

\displaystyle=y_{k,\ell}\quad\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]

\displaystyle z_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle=0

Therefore, (
20
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N},\textbf{z}\end{subarray}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}-z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}}\right)\lambda_{k}

(21)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &z_{\left\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,L}\right\}}\leq 1&&\forall k\in[K],L\in[L_{k}]\\
&x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\\
&0\leq z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}-z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}&&\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]\\
&z_{\emptyset}=0\end{aligned}

It follows from the definitions of
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{P}
that the constraints of (
21
) can be written equivalently as

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N},\textbf{z}\end{subarray}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}-z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}}\right)\lambda_{k}

(22)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &z_{E}\leq 1&&\forall E\in\mathscr{E}\\
&x_{i}\leq z_{E\cup\{i\}}&&\forall(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}\\
&0\leq z_{E\cup\{i\}}-z_{E}\leq x_{i}&&\forall(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}\\
&z_{\emptyset}=0\end{aligned}

To conclude the proof of Theorem
3.2
, we reformulate the objective function of (
22
). Indeed, we observe that the objective function of (
22
) satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}-z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}}\right)\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell}\}}-z_{\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}}\right)\lambda_{k}\mathbb{I}\left\{E=\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}\text{ and }i=i_{k,\ell}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{E\cup\{i\}}-z_{E}\right)\lambda_{k}\mathbb{I}\left\{E=\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}\text{ and }i=i_{k,\ell}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{E\cup\{i\}}-z_{E}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\mathbb{I}\left\{E=\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,\ell-1}\}\text{ and }i=i_{k,\ell}\right\}\lambda_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{E\cup\{i\}}-z_{E}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{I}\left\{E=\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,|E|}\}\text{ and }i=i_{k,|E|+1}\right\}\lambda_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(E,i)\in\mathscr{P}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}\left(z_{E\cup\{i\}}-z_{E}\right)\lambda_{E,i}

where first equality follows from the definition of
\mathscr{P}
, the second, third, and fourth equalities follow from algebra, and the fifth equality is the definition of
\lambda_{E,i}
. That concludes our proof of Theorem
3.2
.

\square

B.4
Proof of Proposition
3.3

Proof B.5

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.
We recall that the exclusion set formulation (
12
) is equivalent to (
11
) (Theorem
3.2
), and we recall from Section
3.1.2
that (
11
) is stronger than (
8
). Therefore, to prove Proposition
3.3
, it suffices to show that (
8
) is integral in the case where we have a cardinality constraint that is equal to one. Indeed, consider the formulation (
8
) with this cardinality constraint:

\displaystyle\underset{{\bm{x}}\in\{0,1\}^{N},{\bm{y}}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}y_{k,\ell}\lambda_{k}

(23)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}y_{k,\ell}\leq 1

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K]

\displaystyle x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]

\displaystyle 0\leq y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}\leq 1

We now consider the linear programming relaxation of (
23
). Indeed, it follows from the fact that
r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}>0
that there exists an optimal solution for the linear programming relaxation that satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}=1
. Now consider any
{\bm{x}}\in[0,1]^{N}
that satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}=1
. It follows from the constraints
y_{k,\ell}\leq x_{i_{k,\ell}}
and from the fact that
r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}>0
that an upper bound on (
23
) is achieved by letting
y_{k,\ell}=x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
k\in[K]
and
\ell\in[L_{k}]
, i.e., the optimal objective value of the linear programming relaxation of (
23
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}x_{i_{k,\ell}}\lambda_{k}

We also observe that letting
y_{k,\ell}=x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for each ranking
k\in[K]
and
\ell\in[L_{k}]
is a feasible solution for the linear programming relaxation of (
23
), since

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}y_{k,\ell}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq 1

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K]

\displaystyle x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}x_{i_{k,\ell^{\prime}}}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}y_{k,\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]

\displaystyle 0\leq y_{k,\ell}=x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq 1

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\ell\in[L_{k}]

Therefore, the optimal objective value of (
23
) is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}x_{i_{k,\ell}}\lambda_{k}

Since the above reasoning holds for all
{\bm{x}}\in[0,1]^{N}
that satisfy
\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}=1
, we conclude that the linear programming relaxation of (
23
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\underset{{\bm{x}}\in[0,1]^{N}:\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}\leq 1}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}r_{i_{k,\ell}}x_{i_{k,\ell}}\lambda_{k}

(24)

Because there exists an optimal solution for (
24
) that is integral, we conclude that the linear programming relaxations of (
12
) and (
8
) are equivalent and integral when we have a cardinality constraint of a single product.
\halmos

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Our proof of Theorem
4.3
is split into the following two theorems.

Theorem C.1

If
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
, then there exists
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
that satisfies

\displaystyle J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})=\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}\quad\forall{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}

Theorem C.2

If
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
, then there exists
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
that satisfies

\displaystyle J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\leq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}\quad\forall{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}

The proofs of Theorems
C.1
and
C.2
are found in Appendices
C.1
and
C.2
. For the sake of completeness, we show in Appendix
C.3
that Theorem
4.3
follows from Theorems
C.1
and
C.2
.

C.1
Proof of Theorem
C.1

Let
\bar{r}^{*}\triangleq\max_{i\in[N+1]}r_{i}
denote the maximum revenue among all products.
Our proof of Theorem
C.1
is constructive. Indeed, consider any
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
, and define
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\},&\text{if }\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}+1\},\\
0,&\text{if }\ell\in\{L_{k}+2,\ldots,N+1\}\end{cases}

(25a)

\displaystyle\beta_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell},&\text{if }\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}\},\\
\bar{r}^{*}-\delta_{L_{k}+1},&\text{if }\ell=L_{k}+1,\\
0,&\text{if }\ell\in\{L_{k}+2,\ldots,N+1\}\end{cases}

(25b)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{*}

(25c)

In what follows, we complete the proof of Theorem
C.1
by showing that the solution
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
constructed in (
25
) satisfies
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
as well as satisfies
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})=\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. We prove these two results in the following Propositions
C.3
and
C.5
.

Proposition C.3

({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
.

Proof C.4

Proof of Proposition
C.3
.
Our proof consists of showing that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
satisfies the constraints of the linear program (
7
). Indeed, for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}+1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma+\alpha_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\alpha_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{L_{k}}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}-\beta_{L_{k}+1}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{*}+\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{L_{k}}\left(\delta_{\ell^{\prime}+1}-\delta_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)-\left(\bar{r}^{*}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right)-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}0

\displaystyle=\delta_{\ell}+\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq r_{i_{k,\ell}}

(26)

The first equality follows from algebra. The second equality follows from our construction of
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
. The third equality from canceling terms. The inequality follows from algebra. Moreover, for
\ell\in\{L_{k}+2,\ldots,N+1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma+\alpha_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{*}+0-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}0\geq r_{i_{k,\ell}}

(27)

The first equality follows from our construction of
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
, and the inequality follows from the definition of
\bar{r}^{*}
.
Combining (
26
) and (
27
), we have shown that

\displaystyle\gamma+\alpha_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}\geq r_{i_{k,\ell}}\quad\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,N+1\}

It is straightforward to show that
\alpha_{\ell}\geq 0
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,N+1\}
. Moreover, for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,N+1\}
,

\displaystyle\beta_{\ell}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}\delta_{\ell}-\delta_{\ell},&\text{if }\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}\},\\
\bar{r}^{*}-\delta_{L_{k}+1},&\text{if }\ell=L_{k}+1,\\
0,&\text{if }\ell\in\{L_{k}+2,\ldots,N+1\}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\geq 0

where the first inequality follows from the fact that
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
, and the second inequality follows from algebra and from the definition of
\bar{r}^{*}
. We have thus shown that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
satisfies the constraints of the linear program (
7
), which implies that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
. Our proof of Proposition
C.3
is thus complete.

\halmos

Proposition C.5

J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})=\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}

Proof C.6

Proof of Proposition
C.5
.
Consider any
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. For each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}\}
, it follows from our construction of
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
that

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

For the case of
\ell=L_{k}+1
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,L_{k}+1}}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right\}-\left(\bar{r}^{*}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,L_{k}+1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\max\left\{0,r_{N+1}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right\}-\left(\bar{r}^{*}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right)\right)x_{N+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\max\left\{0,0-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right\}-\left(\bar{r}^{*}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right)\right)1

\displaystyle=\delta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}^{*}

The first equality follows from our construction of
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
. The second equality follows from algebra and from the fact that
i_{k,L_{k}+1}=N+1
. The third equality follows from the fact that
r_{N+1}=0
and from the fact that
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
(which implies that
x_{N+1}=1
). The fourth equality follows from the fact that
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
(which implies that
\delta_{L_{k}+1}\geq 0
). For each
\ell\in\left\{L_{k}+2,\ldots,N+2\right\}
, it follows from our construction of
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
that

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\left(0-0\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}=0

We have thus shown that

\displaystyle\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}+\left(\alpha_{L_{k}+1}-\beta_{L_{k}+1}\right)x_{i_{k,L_{k}+1}}+\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{*}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}+\left(\delta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}^{*}\right)+\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}0

\displaystyle=\delta_{L_{k}+1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

which completes our proof of Proposition
C.5
.

\halmos

C.2
Proof of Theorem
C.2

Our proof of Theorem
C.2
is constructive. Indeed, consider any
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
, and define
{\bm{\delta}}
as

\displaystyle\delta_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\min\left\{\gamma-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}},\bar{r}_{k}\right\},0\right\}\quad\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}+1\}

(28)

In what follows, we complete the proof of Theorem
C.2
by showing that the solution
{\bm{\delta}}
constructed in (
28
) satisfies
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
as well as satisfies
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\leq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. We prove these two results in the following Propositions
C.7
and
C.9
.

Proposition C.7

{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
.

Proof C.8

Proof of Proposition
C.7
.
It follows immediately from (
28
) that
\delta_{1}\geq 0
and that
\delta_{L_{k}+1}\leq\bar{r}_{k}
. Moreover, for each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}\}
, it follows from (
28
) that

\displaystyle\delta_{\ell+1}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\min\left\{\gamma-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}+\beta_{\ell+1},\bar{r}_{k}\right\},0\right\}\geq\max\left\{\min\left\{\gamma-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}},\bar{r}_{k}\right\},0\right\}=\delta_{\ell}

where the inequality follows from the fact that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
, which implies that
\beta_{\ell+1}\geq 0
. We have thus shown that
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
.

\halmos

Proposition C.9

J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\leq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
.

Proof C.10

Proof of Proposition
C.9
.
Consider any
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. It follows from fact that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
that

\displaystyle\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}\geq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\left(\gamma-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}\right),0\right\}-\beta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

(29)

For notational convenience, define

\displaystyle\zeta_{\ell}\triangleq\gamma-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{N+1}\beta_{\ell^{\prime}}\quad\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,N+2\}

It follows from the above definition that
\beta_{\ell}=\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,N+1\}
and that

\displaystyle\delta_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\min\left\{\zeta_{\ell},\bar{r}_{k}\right\},0\right\}\quad\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}+1\}

(30)

In view of the above notation and (
29
), to complete the proof of Proposition
C.9
, it suffices for us to show that

\displaystyle\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}\geq J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})

(31)

Our proof of (
31
) will make use of the following Claims
3
and
4
.

Claim 3

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\geq-\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}

Proof C.11

Proof of Claim
3
.
Consider any
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}\}
. We observe that

\displaystyle\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)-\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)-\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}-\left(\bar{r}_{k}-\bar{r}_{k}\right)\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)-\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right),&\text{if }0\leq\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k},\\
\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)-\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-0,0\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-0\right)\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}<0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell+1}\right),&\text{if }0\leq\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell+1}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}<0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell+1}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell+1}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k}<\zeta_{\ell+1},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell+1}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\zeta_{\ell+1}\leq\bar{r}_{k}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\bar{r}_{k}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k}<\zeta_{\ell+1},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell+1},0\right\}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\zeta_{\ell+1}\leq\bar{r}_{k}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\bar{r}_{k}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k}<\zeta_{\ell+1},\\
\max\left\{0,-\zeta_{\ell+1}\right\},&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\zeta_{\ell+1}\leq\bar{r}_{k}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}>\bar{r}_{k},\\
-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\bar{r}_{k}\right),&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\bar{r}_{k}<\zeta_{\ell+1},\\
0,&\text{if }\zeta_{\ell}\leq\zeta_{\ell+1}\leq\bar{r}_{k}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=-\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}-\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}\right)

(32)

The first equality follows from (
30
). The second equality follows from algebra and the definition of
\bar{r}_{k}
. The third and fourth equalities follow from algebra. The fifth equality follows from (
30
). The sixth equality, the inequality, and the seventh equality follow from algebra. Using the above inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\geq-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}-\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\geq-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}-\max\left\{\zeta_{\ell}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}\right)1

\displaystyle=-\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}-\max\left\{\zeta_{1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}

The first inequality follows from (
32
). The second inequality follows from the fact that
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
(which implies that
x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq 1
) and the fact that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
(which implies that
\zeta_{\ell+1}\geq\zeta_{\ell}
). The equality follows from algebra, and the third inequality follows from algebra. This completes our proof of Claim
3
.

\halmos

Claim 4

\displaystyle\gamma+\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+1}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\geq\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}

Proof C.12

Proof of Claim
4
.
We observe that

\displaystyle\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,L_{k}+1}}-\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{L_{k}+2}-\zeta_{L_{k}+1}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,L_{k}+1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\max\left\{0-\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{L_{k}+2}-\zeta_{L_{k}+1}\right)\right)1

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\zeta_{L_{k}+2}

(33)

The first equality follows from the definition of
L_{k}
and from the fact that
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
(which implies that
x_{i_{k,L_{k}+1}}=1
), and the second equality follows from algebra. Therefore,

\displaystyle\gamma+\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+1}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\zeta_{L_{k}+2}\right)+\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}

\displaystyle\geq\gamma+\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\zeta_{L_{k}+2}\right)-\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}

\displaystyle\geq\gamma+\left(\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\zeta_{L_{k}+2}\right)-\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+2}^{N+1}\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)1-\delta_{L_{k}+1}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\zeta_{N+2}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\min\left\{\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\},\bar{r}_{k}\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1},0\right\}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}

The first equality follows from (
33
). The first inequality follows from algebra and from the fact that
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
(which implies that
x_{i_{k,\ell}}\geq 0
). The second inequality follows from the fact that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
(which implies that
\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\geq 0
) and the fact that
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
(which implies that
x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq 1
). The second equality follows from algebra. The third equality follows from the fact that
\zeta_{N+2}=\gamma
. The fourth equality follows from (
30
). The fifth and sixth equalities follow from algebra. Our proof of Claim
4
is complete.

\halmos

Combining Claims
3
and
4
, we have shown that

\displaystyle\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma+\sum_{\ell=L_{k}+1}^{N+1}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{L_{k}+1}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\zeta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\zeta_{\ell+1}-\zeta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell},0\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}-\max\left\{\zeta_{L_{k}+1}-\bar{r}_{k},0\right\}

\displaystyle=0

where first equality follows from algebra and the definition of
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})
, the inequality follows from Claims
3
and
4
, and the second equality follows from algebra. We have thus shown that (
31
) holds, which completes our proof of Proposition
C.9
.
\halmos

C.3
Proof of Theorem
4.3

For the sake of completeness, we conclude Appendix
C
by showing that Theorem
4.3
follows from Theorems
C.1
and
C.2
.

Proof C.13

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.
We first show that every optimal solution for (
13
) is an optimal solution for (
7
). Indeed, it follows from Theorem
C.1
that every optimal solution for (
13
) can be transformed into a feasible (but possibly suboptimal) solution for (
7
) with the same objective value. This implies that the optimal objective value of (
13
) is greater than or equal to the optimal objective value of (
7
). Moreover, it follows from Theorem
C.2
that every optimal solution for (
7
) can be transformed into a feasible (but possibly suboptimal) solution for (
13
) with the same or better objective value. This implies that the optimal objective value of (
7
) is greater than or equal to the optimal objective value of (
13
). We thus conclude that the optimal objective values of (
7
) and (
13
) are equal, and that every optimal solution for (
13
) can be transformed into an optimal solution for (
7
).

Consider any
{\bm{\delta}}\in\Delta_{k}
that is a Pareto cut in the sense of Definition
4.2
. It follows from Theorem
C.1
that there exists
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
that satisfies
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})=\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell})x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. Now suppose for the sake of developing a contradiction that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
is
not
a Pareto cut in the sense of Definition
4.1
. Then it follows from that supposition that there exists
({\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime},{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
that dominates
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
. It follows from Theorem
C.2
that there exists
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{k}
that satisfies that satisfies
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})\leq\gamma^{\prime}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}(\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell}-\beta^{\prime}_{\ell})x_{i_{k,\ell}}
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. We have thus shown that

\displaystyle J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})\leq\gamma^{\prime}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}(\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell}-\beta^{\prime}_{\ell})x_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\gamma+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N+1}(\alpha_{\ell}-\beta_{\ell})x_{i_{k,\ell}}=J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})\;\;\text{for all }{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}

where the second inequality is strict at some
\bar{{\bm{x}}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
. We have thus shown that
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
dominates
{\bm{\delta}}
, which contradicts the assumption that
{\bm{\delta}}
is a Pareto cut. This contradiction implies that
({\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\gamma)
is a Pareto cut in the sense of Definition
4.1
, which completes the proof of Theorem
4.3
.

\halmos

Appendix D
Proof of Proposition
4.4

Proof D.1

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.
Let
k\in[K]
and
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
. We observe that
{\bm{\delta}}
is an optimal solution for (
13
) because

\displaystyle J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})

\displaystyle=\delta_{L_{k}+1}+\sum_{\ell=\ell^{*}}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\delta_{\ell}\right\}-\left(\delta_{\ell+1}-\delta_{\ell}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{k}+\sum_{\ell=\ell^{*}+1}^{L_{k}}\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell}}-\bar{r}_{k}\right\}-\left(\bar{r}_{k}-\bar{r}_{k}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\max\left\{0,r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}-r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}\right\}-\left(\bar{r}_{k}-r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{k}+\sum_{\ell=\ell^{*}+1}^{L_{k}}\left(0-0\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}+\left(0-\left(\bar{r}_{k}-r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}\right)\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{k}-\left(\bar{r}_{k}-r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}\right)x_{i_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle=r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}

The first equality holds because our definition of
\ell^{*}
in (
14
) implies that
x_{i_{k,1}}=\cdots=x_{i_{k,\ell^{*}-1}}=0
. The second equality follows from our construction of
{\bm{\delta}}
in (
15
). The third equality follows from the fact that
r_{i_{k,\ell}}\leq\bar{r}_{k}
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L_{k}+1\}
. The fourth equality follows from algebra. The fifth equality holds because our definition of
\ell^{*}
in (
14
) implies that
x_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}=1
. Since the optimal objective value of (
13
) is clearly equal to
r_{i_{k,\ell^{*}}}
, our proof of Proposition
4.4
is complete.

\halmos

Appendix E
Illustration for Properties
7
,
7
,
7
, and
7

In this appendix, we provide intuition for Properties
7
,
7
,
7
, and
7
by presenting four illustrative examples. Each example shows that removing any one of those four properties from Section
4.3
can allow for solutions for (
13
) that are not Pareto cuts, in the sense of Definition
4.2
. By showing how removing any of those properties allows for non-Pareto cuts, we motivate each of the four properties as well as motivate the four subroutines in the transformation algorithm from Section
4.4
.

Example E.1

(Property
7
is violated)

Suppose that there are
N=2
products, the revenues are
r_{1}=\$10
and
r_{2}=\$5
, and ranking
k
satisfies
i_{k,1}=1
,
i_{k,2}=2
, and
i_{k,3}=3
. Consider the two feasible solutions
{\bm{\delta}}=(10,10,10)
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}=(5,5,5)
, which correspond to

\displaystyle J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})

\displaystyle=10+(\max\{0,10-10\}-(10-10))x_{1}+(\max\{0,5-10\}-(10-10))x_{2}

\displaystyle=10

\displaystyle J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=5+(\max\{0,10-5\}-(5-5))x_{1}+(\max\{0,5-5\}-(5-5))x_{2}

\displaystyle=5+5x_{1}

We observe that
{\bm{\delta}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
are optimal solutions for (
13
) in the case of
{\bm{x}}=(1,0,1)
. We also observe that
T_{k}({\bm{\delta}})=1
(since
\delta_{1}=10=r_{i_{k,1}}
,
\delta_{2}=10>r_{i_{k,2}}=5
, and
\delta_{3}=10>r_{i_{k,1}}=0
), and that
T_{k}({\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})=2
(since
\delta_{2}^{\prime}=5=r_{i_{k,2}}
and
\delta_{3}^{\prime}=5>r_{i_{k,3}}=0
).

•

It follows from the fact that
T_{k}({\bm{\delta}})=1
that
{\bm{\delta}}
does not satisfy Property
7
, and it follows from the fact that
T_{k}({\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})=2
that
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
satisfies Property
7
.

•

It follows from the fact that
\delta_{1}=10=r_{i_{k,1}}
and
\delta_{1}^{\prime}=5\leq r_{i_{k,1}}
that
{\bm{\delta}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
satisfy Property
7
.

•

It follows from the fact that
\delta_{2}=\delta_{3}
and
\delta_{2}^{\prime}=\delta_{3}^{\prime}
that
{\bm{\delta}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
satisfy Property
7
.

•

It follows from the fact that
T_{k}({\bm{\delta}})=1
and
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=\delta_{3}
that
{\bm{\delta}}
satisfies Property
7
, and it follows from the fact that
T_{k}({\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})=2
and
\delta_{2}^{\prime}=\delta_{3}^{\prime}
that
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
satisfies Property
7
.

Finally, we observe that
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})=J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
that satisfy
x_{1}=1
and that
J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime})<J_{k}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\delta}})
for all
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}^{c}
that satisfy
x_{1}\in[0,1)
. We have thus shown that
{\bm{\delta}}^{\prime}
dominates
{\bm{\delta}}
.
\halmos
